========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, hello, doctor. It's nice to meet you, too. I... well, I came in today because I just can’t seem to get anything in order anymore. It's really gotten completely out of hand. Just to give you an example, if you were to walk into my house right now and look at my kitchen table, you wouldn't even be able to see the wood underneath. It is just completely covered in piles. 

I mean, there are my husband’s appointment papers—he's had quite a few lately, and they always hand you those thick printouts with the instructions and the dates, and I bring them home and just set them down. And then right on top of those are all the church flyers. I try to keep up with the Sunday social hour, so I have all these bulletins and announcements. Oh, and the senior-center notes! They send out these newsletters and activity calendars, and I keep meaning to go through them, but they just pile up right alongside all these half-finished lists I make. I'm always starting lists on the backs of envelopes to try and remember what to do, but I never finish them. 

And the worst part, the absolute most frustrating part, is that I will pull out a chair and sit there at that table, looking at this mountain of paper, and I just... I don't know which thing to do first. I sit there trying to figure out where to start, and I get so overwhelmed that I just stare at it, or I shuffle a few things around, and before I know it, the sun is going down and the whole day is just gone. Completely gone. And I just sit there feeling terrible about it, but I don't know how to fix it.

Oh, goodness, no. No, it's not just the last few years at all. It feels like it’s been my entire life, really. I mean, my husband thinks it’s just me getting older, you know, because when you reach seventy you expect some things to slip a little bit. But if I’m being completely honest with myself—and with you—it’s always been exactly like this. 

I was actually just thinking about this the other day. I was going through an old cedar chest in the spare bedroom, trying to find a specific photo album, and I happened to come across a stack of my old report cards from elementary school. My mother had saved every single one of them in this little tied-up bundle. And I sat on the edge of the bed reading them, and it was just... well, it brought tears to my eyes, honestly, because it was exactly the same struggle. Even as a little girl, my teachers would write these notes to my parents, and they always said the exact same things. They wrote that I was a bright girl, very capable, but just so careless. That was the word they always used: "careless." 

I was constantly forgetting my reading books, or I would actually do my homework and then leave it sitting right on the counter at home, which used to drive my mother up the wall. And the seatwork... oh, my goodness. We would have these little worksheets to do at our desks, and I would just sit there looking out the window, watching the other children or just daydreaming, and the teacher would always have to come over and tap on my desk. I needed constant reminders to just finish my seatwork, because if I didn't finish it, I wasn't allowed to go outside for recess. I would just sit there at that little wooden desk with the inkwell hole, feeling so frustrated because I desperately wanted to go outside and play, but I just couldn't seem to get my brain to focus on finishing the paper in front of me. 

So, no, doctor, it’s definitely not a new thing. It’s just... I suppose the piles are just different now. Instead of schoolwork, it's church bulletins. But the feeling of sitting there and not knowing how to start is exactly the same.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, you know, it's funny you ask that, because I think people always assume that little girls are perfectly still and quiet, but I... well, I was never really the type to be jumping off the furniture or running around the classroom, if that's what you mean. I wasn't getting up out of my seat and causing a ruckus like some of the little boys did. But still? Oh, I don't think I've ever been truly still a day in my life. 

Even now, at my age, I just cannot seem to sit quietly. I go to these committee meetings at the church—I'm on the altar guild, and we have these long meetings to discuss the flower arrangements and the linens, and they can go on for an hour or more. And I try, I really do try to just sit politely with my hands folded in my lap like a proper lady. But before I even realize I'm doing it, I'll catch myself rubbing my hands together over and over, or I'll be tapping my foot against the leg of the chair. Tap, tap, tap. And if I have a pen in my hand to take notes? Forget it. I'll be clicking the top of it, or rolling it between my fingers, or taking the cap off and putting it back on. 

And I shift in my chair constantly. I'll cross my legs, and then uncross them, and then lean to one side, and then adjust my skirt, and then lean forward. I just get this... this restless feeling in my body, like my muscles just can't settle down. And I know it's noticeable, because sometimes the other ladies will stop talking and look over at me, and I'll realize I've been jiggling my foot so hard that the whole table is shaking. It's terribly embarrassing, really. You'd think a seventy-year-old woman would know how to sit still through a meeting, but I just can't seem to manage it. I try to stop, I'll tell myself, "Just be still," but then five minutes later, my foot is going again. It's like I have a little motor inside me that just never shuts off, even when I'm supposed to be perfectly still.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, hello, doctor. It's nice to meet you, too. I... well, I came in today because I just can’t seem to get anything in order anymore. It's really gotten completely out of hand. Just to give you an example, if you were to walk into my house right now and look at my kitchen table, you wouldn't even be able to see the wood underneath. It is just completely covered in piles. \n\nI mean, there are my husband’s appointment papers—he's had quite a few lately, and they always hand you those thick printouts with the instructions and the dates, and I bring them home and just set them down. And then right on top of those are all the church flyers. I try to keep up with the Sunday social hour, so I have all these bulletins and announcements. Oh, and the senior-center notes! They send out these newsletters and activity calendars, and I keep meaning to go through them, but they just pile up right alongside all these half-finished lists I make. I'm always starting lists on the backs of envelopes to try and remember what to do, but I never finish them. \n\nAnd the worst part, the absolute most frustrating part, is that I will pull out a chair and sit there at that table, looking at this mountain of paper, and I just... I don't know which thing to do first. I sit there trying to figure out where to start, and I get so overwhelmed that I just stare at it, or I shuffle a few things around, and before I know it, the sun is going down and the whole day is just gone. Completely gone. And I just sit there feeling terrible about it, but I don't know how to fix it.\n\nDoctor: Has this trouble getting things organized and knowing where to start come on only in the last few years?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. No, it's not just the last few years at all. It feels like it’s been my entire life, really. I mean, my husband thinks it’s just me getting older, you know, because when you reach seventy you expect some things to slip a little bit. But if I’m being completely honest with myself—and with you—it’s always been exactly like this. \n\nI was actually just thinking about this the other day. I was going through an old cedar chest in the spare bedroom, trying to find a specific photo album, and I happened to come across a stack of my old report cards from elementary school. My mother had saved every single one of them in this little tied-up bundle. And I sat on the edge of the bed reading them, and it was just... well, it brought tears to my eyes, honestly, because it was exactly the same struggle. Even as a little girl, my teachers would write these notes to my parents, and they always said the exact same things. They wrote that I was a bright girl, very capable, but just so careless. That was the word they always used: \"careless.\" \n\nI was constantly forgetting my reading books, or I would actually do my homework and then leave it sitting right on the counter at home, which used to drive my mother up the wall. And the seatwork... oh, my goodness. We would have these little worksheets to do at our desks, and I would just sit there looking out the window, watching the other children or just daydreaming, and the teacher would always have to come over and tap on my desk. I needed constant reminders to just finish my seatwork, because if I didn't finish it, I wasn't allowed to go outside for recess. I would just sit there at that little wooden desk with the inkwell hole, feeling so frustrated because I desperately wanted to go outside and play, but I just couldn't seem to get my brain to focus on finishing the paper in front of me. \n\nSo, no, doctor, it’s definitely not a new thing. It’s just... I suppose the piles are just different now. Instead of schoolwork, it's church bulletins. But the feeling of sitting there and not knowing how to start is exactly the same.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, were you often fidgety or unable to stay seated when you were supposed to?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's funny you ask that, because I think people always assume that little girls are perfectly still and quiet, but I... well, I was never really the type to be jumping off the furniture or running around the classroom, if that's what you mean. I wasn't getting up out of my seat and causing a ruckus like some of the little boys did. But still? Oh, I don't think I've ever been truly still a day in my life. \n\nEven now, at my age, I just cannot seem to sit quietly. I go to these committee meetings at the church—I'm on the altar guild, and we have these long meetings to discuss the flower arrangements and the linens, and they can go on for an hour or more. And I try, I really do try to just sit politely with my hands folded in my lap like a proper lady. But before I even realize I'm doing it, I'll catch myself rubbing my hands together over and over, or I'll be tapping my foot against the leg of the chair. Tap, tap, tap. And if I have a pen in my hand to take notes? Forget it. I'll be clicking the top of it, or rolling it between my fingers, or taking the cap off and putting it back on. \n\nAnd I shift in my chair constantly. I'll cross my legs, and then uncross them, and then lean to one side, and then adjust my skirt, and then lean forward. I just get this... this restless feeling in my body, like my muscles just can't settle down. And I know it's noticeable, because sometimes the other ladies will stop talking and look over at me, and I'll realize I've been jiggling my foot so hard that the whole table is shaking. It's terribly embarrassing, really. You'd think a seventy-year-old woman would know how to sit still through a meeting, but I just can't seem to manage it. I try to stop, I'll tell myself, \"Just be still,\" but then five minutes later, my foot is going again. It's like I have a little motor inside me that just never shuts off, even when I'm supposed to be perfectly still.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I'll cross my legs, and then uncross them, and then lean to one side, and then adjust my skirt, and then lean forward. I just get this... this restless feeling in my body, like my muscles just can't settle down. And I know it's noticeable, because sometimes the other ladies will stop talking and look over at me, and I'll realize I've been jiggling my foot so hard that the whole table is shaking. It's terribly embarrassing, really. You'd think a seventy-year-old woman would know how to sit still through a meeting, but I just can't seem to manage it. I try to stop, I'll tell myself, \"Just be still,\" but then five minutes later, my foot is going again. It's like I have a little motor inside me that just never shuts off, even when I'm supposed to be perfectly still.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave these same problems caused trouble for you in other parts of your life too, like work, managing the household, or relationships?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 70 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I had to give up leading a big senior-center event, and my daughter has started double-checking bills and appointments because she no longer trusts that I can keep everything straight on my own.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, yes. Oh, it really has. It breaks my heart, it truly does, because I’ve always tried to be so involved and useful, but it feels like it's just ruining everything lately. 

I mean... just to give you an example, just last month, I was supposed to be in charge of this big event at the senior center. It was their annual Spring Jubilee luncheon, which is a lovely gathering, we do a silent auction and a big catered meal to raise money for the community pantry. I was so honored when they asked me to chair the committee. But... well, I had all the vendor contacts, and the volunteer sign-up sheets, and the donation forms, and I just couldn't organize any of it. The papers were scattered all over the house, just like my kitchen table. I missed the deadline to order the centerpieces, and I kept forgetting to call the caterer back to confirm the final headcount because I couldn't find the list of who had RSVP'd. It got so chaotic, and I was so paralyzed by all the details, that I actually had to step down. I had to call the director and give up leading the whole event completely. It was just humiliating to admit I couldn't handle it. I felt like I let all those lovely people down.

And it’s taking a toll on my family, too. Especially with my daughter, Sarah. She’s such a sweetheart, she really is, and she works so hard at her own job, but she's had to start stepping in to manage me, basically. It started a little while ago when a couple of important notices got buried under those church bulletins I mentioned, and now... well, now she comes over every single Sunday afternoon. She says she's just coming to visit and have tea, but I know exactly what she's really doing. She sits down at that messy table and she double-checks all of our bills and goes through every single one of my husband's and my appointments. She literally takes my calendar and cross-references it to make sure I haven't messed up the dates or forgotten to mail a check. 

She does it because she just doesn’t trust that I can keep everything straight on my own anymore. And I can't even be angry with her for interfering, because she's right! I *can't* keep it straight. But it just feels awful, you know? To be seventy years old and have your own child double-checking your mail because you're too scatterbrained to run your own household. I used to manage everything when she was a little girl, and now I can't even be trusted to pay the water bill on time without supervision. It just feels like this... this inability to organize anything is just bleeding into absolutely every part of my life.

And then right on top of those are all the church flyers. I try to keep up with the Sunday social hour, so I have all these bulletins and announcements. Oh, and the senior-center notes! I'm always starting lists on the backs of envelopes to try and remember what to do, but I never finish them. \n\nAnd the worst part, the absolute most frustrating part, is that I will pull out a chair and sit there at that table, looking at this mountain of paper, and I just... I don't know which thing to do first. I sit there trying to figure out where to start, and I get so overwhelmed that I just stare at it, or I shuffle a few things around, and before I know it, the sun is going down and the whole day is just gone. Completely gone. And I just sit there feeling terrible about it, but I don't know how to fix it.\n\nDoctor: Has this trouble getting things organized and knowing where to start come on only in the last few years?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. No, it's not just the last few years at all. It feels like it’s been my entire life, really. I mean, my husband thinks it’s just me getting older, you know, because when you reach seventy you expect some things to slip a little bit. But if I’m being completely honest with myself—and with you—it’s always been exactly like this. \n\nI was actually just thinking about this the other day. I was going through an old cedar chest in the spare bedroom, trying to find a specific photo album, and I happened to come across a stack of my old report cards from elementary school. My mother had saved every single one of them in this little tied-up bundle. And I sat on the edge of the bed reading them, and it was just... well, it brought tears to my eyes, honestly, because it was exactly the same struggle. Even as a little girl, my teachers would write these notes to my parents, and they always said the exact same things. They wrote that I was a bright girl, very capable, but just so careless. That was the word they always used: \"careless.\" \n\nI was constantly forgetting my reading books, or I would actually do my homework and then leave it sitting right on the counter at home, which used to drive my mother up the wall. We would have these little worksheets to do at our desks, and I would just sit there looking out the window, watching the other children or just daydreaming, and the teacher would always have to come over and tap on my desk. I needed constant reminders to just finish my seatwork, because if I didn't finish it, I wasn't allowed to go outside for recess. I would just sit there at that little wooden desk with the inkwell hole, feeling so frustrated because I desperately wanted to go outside and play, but I just couldn't seem to get my brain to focus on finishing the paper in front of me. \n\nSo, no, doctor, it’s definitely not a new thing. It’s just... I suppose the piles are just different now. Instead of schoolwork, it's church bulletins. But the feeling of sitting there and not knowing how to start is exactly the same.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, were you often fidgety or unable to stay seated when you were supposed to?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's funny you ask that, because I think people always assume that little girls are perfectly still and quiet, but I... well, I was never really the type to be jumping off the furniture or running around the classroom, if that's what you mean. I wasn't getting up out of my seat and causing a ruckus like some of the little boys did. But still? Oh, I don't think I've ever been truly still a day in my life. \n\nEven now, at my age, I just cannot seem to sit quietly. I go to these committee meetings at the church—I'm on the altar guild, and we have these long meetings to discuss the flower arrangements and the linens, and they can go on for an hour or more. I try to stop, I'll tell myself, \"Just be still,\" but then five minutes later, my foot is going again. Even as a little girl, my teachers would write these notes to my parents, and they always said the exact same things. They wrote that I was a bright girl, very capable, but just so careless. That was the word they always used: \"careless.\" \n\nI was constantly forgetting my reading books, or I would actually do my homework and then leave it sitting right on the counter at home, which used to drive my mother up the wall. And the seatwork... oh, my goodness. We would have these little worksheets to do at our desks, and I would just sit there looking out the window, watching the other children or just daydreaming, and the teacher would always have to come over and tap on my desk. I needed constant reminders to just finish my seatwork, because if I didn't finish it, I wasn't allowed to go outside for recess. I would just sit there at that little wooden desk with the inkwell hole, feeling so frustrated because I desperately wanted to go outside and play, but I just couldn't seem to get my brain to focus on finishing the paper in front of me. \n\nSo, no, doctor, it’s definitely not a new thing. It’s just... I suppose the piles are just different now. Instead of schoolwork, it's church bulletins. But the feeling of sitting there and not knowing how to start is exactly the same.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, were you often fidgety or unable to stay seated when you were supposed to?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's funny you ask that, because I think people always assume that little girls are perfectly still and quiet, but I... well, I was never really the type to be jumping off the furniture or running around the classroom, if that's what you mean. I wasn't getting up out of my seat and causing a ruckus like some of the little boys did. But still? Oh, I don't think I've ever been truly still a day in my life. \n\nEven now, at my age, I just cannot seem to sit quietly. I go to these committee meetings at the church—I'm on the altar guild, and we have these long meetings to discuss the flower arrangements and the linens, and they can go on for an hour or more. And I try, I really do try to just sit politely with my hands folded in my lap like a proper lady. But before I even realize I'm doing it, I'll catch myself rubbing my hands together over and over, or I'll be tapping my foot against the leg of the chair. Tap, tap, tap. And if I have a pen in my hand to take notes? Forget it. I'll be clicking the top of it, or rolling it between my fingers, or taking the cap off and putting it back on. \n\nAnd I shift in my chair constantly. I'll cross my legs, and then uncross them, and then lean to one side, and then adjust my skirt, and then lean forward. I just get this... this restless feeling in my body, like my muscles just can't settle down. And I know it's noticeable, because sometimes the other ladies will stop talking and look over at me, and I'll realize I've been jiggling my foot so hard that the whole table is shaking. It's terribly embarrassing, really. You'd think a seventy-year-old woman would know how to sit still through a meeting, but I just can't seem to manage it. I try to stop, I'll tell myself, \"Just be still,\" but then five minutes later, my foot is going again. It's like I have a little motor inside me that just never shuts off, even when I'm supposed to be perfectly still.\n\nDoctor: Have these same problems caused trouble for you in other parts of your life too, like work, managing the household, or relationships?\n\nPatient: Oh, yes. Oh, it really has. It breaks my heart, it truly does, because I’ve always tried to be so involved and useful, but it feels like it's just ruining everything lately. \n\nI mean... just to give you an example, just last month, I was supposed to be in charge of this big event at the senior center. It was their annual Spring Jubilee luncheon, which is a lovely gathering, we do a silent auction and a big catered meal to raise money for the community pantry. I was so honored when they asked me to chair the committee. But... well, I had all the vendor contacts, and the volunteer sign-up sheets, and the donation forms, and I just couldn't organize any of it. The papers were scattered all over the house, just like my kitchen table. I missed the deadline to order the centerpieces, and I kept forgetting to call the caterer back to confirm the final headcount because I couldn't find the list of who had RSVP'd. It got so chaotic, and I was so paralyzed by all the details, that I actually had to step down. I had to call the director and give up leading the whole event completely. It was just humiliating to admit I couldn't handle it. I felt like I let all those lovely people down.\n\nAnd it’s taking a toll on my family, too. Especially with my daughter, Sarah. She’s such a sweetheart, she really is, and she works so hard at her own job, but she's had to start stepping in to manage me, basically. It started a little while ago when a couple of important notices got buried under those church bulletins I mentioned, and now... well, now she comes over every single Sunday afternoon. She says she's just coming to visit and have tea, but I know exactly what she's really doing. She sits down at that messy table and she double-checks all of our bills and goes through every single one of my husband's and my appointments. She literally takes my calendar and cross-references it to make sure I haven't messed up the dates or forgotten to mail a check. \n\nShe does it because she just doesn’t trust that I can keep everything straight on my own anymore. And I can't even be angry with her for interfering, because she's right! I *can't* keep it straight. But it just feels awful, you know? To be seventy years old and have your own child double-checking your mail because you're too scatterbrained to run your own household. I used to manage everything when she was a little girl, and now I can't even be trusted to pay the water bill on time without supervision. It just feels like this... this inability to organize anything is just bleeding into absolutely every part of my life.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "patient_turn_index: 5\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."
  }
]

  "name": "None",

  "reason": "The doctor is asking about feeling down or depressed, which relates to depression, but my symptom profile only includes ADHD symptoms and does not cover mood or depressive symptoms."
}

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, no, no. I wouldn't say that at all. I mean, I get frustrated, certainly. Like I was just saying about Sarah having to check my mail and stepping down from the Spring Jubilee... those things make me feel terribly foolish and upset in the moment. I might even have a little cry about it in the bathroom, just out of pure frustration with myself. But depressed? No, I really don't think so. 

I still wake up looking forward to the day, you know? I still love going out to my garden in the mornings. I have these absolutely beautiful hydrangea bushes that my husband and I planted when we first moved into this house—oh, goodness, it must have been thirty-five years ago now, back when the neighborhood was still mostly empty lots and young families. And I go out there with my coffee every morning and I just love looking at the blooms, deciding which ones to cut to bring inside. And I still enjoy my friends! I meet the girls for lunch every Tuesday at the little bistro downtown, the one that makes that lovely spinach quiche, and we just talk and laugh for hours about everything under the sun. 

So, no, I don't have that dark cloud hanging over me that people talk about when they have real depression. I'm not lying in bed unable to face the day. I *want* to face the day! I want to do all these things and be involved! It's just that when I try to actually do them, I get so terribly tangled up in the details and the paperwork and the organizing. It’s more of a... well, a deep exasperation with my own brain, if that makes sense, rather than a true sadness. I just wish I could be as capable as I feel on the inside, instead of constantly letting things slip through the cracks. But no, doctor, not depressed. Just... very, very annoyed with myself.

It is just completely covered in piles. \n\nI mean, there are my husband’s appointment papers—he's had quite a few lately, and they always hand you those thick printouts with the instructions and the dates, and I bring them home and just set them down. They send out these newsletters and activity calendars, and I keep meaning to go through them, but they just pile up right alongside all these half-finished lists I make. I'm always starting lists on the backs of envelopes to try and remember what to do, but I never finish them. \n\nAnd the worst part, the absolute most frustrating part, is that I will pull out a chair and sit there at that table, looking at this mountain of paper, and I just... I don't know which thing to do first. I sit there trying to figure out where to start, and I get so overwhelmed that I just stare at it, or I shuffle a few things around, and before I know it, the sun is going down and the whole day is just gone. Completely gone. And I just sit there feeling terrible about it, but I don't know how to fix it.\n\nDoctor: Has this trouble getting things organized and knowing where to start come on only in the last few years?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. No, it's not just the last few years at all. It feels like it’s been my entire life, really. I mean, my husband thinks it’s just me getting older, you know, because when you reach seventy you expect some things to slip a little bit. But if I’m being completely honest with myself—and with you—it’s always been exactly like this. \n\nI was actually just thinking about this the other day. I was going through an old cedar chest in the spare bedroom, trying to find a specific photo album, and I happened to come across a stack of my old report cards from elementary school. My mother had saved every single one of them in this little tied-up bundle. And I sat on the edge of the bed reading them, and it was just... well, it brought tears to my eyes, honestly, because it was exactly the same struggle. Even as a little girl, my teachers would write these notes to my parents, and they always said the exact same things. They wrote that I was a bright girl, very capable, but just so careless. That was the word they always used: \"careless.\" \n\nI was constantly forgetting my reading books, or I would actually do my homework and then leave it sitting right on the counter at home, which used to drive my mother up the wall. And the seatwork... oh, my goodness. We would have these little worksheets to do at our desks, and I would just sit there looking out the window, watching the other children or just daydreaming, and the teacher would always have to come over and tap on my desk. I needed constant reminders to just finish my seatwork, because if I didn't finish it, I wasn't allowed to go outside for recess. I would just sit there at that little wooden desk with the inkwell hole, feeling so frustrated because I desperately wanted to go outside and play, but I just couldn't seem to get my brain to focus on finishing the paper in front of me. \n\nSo, no, doctor, it’s definitely not a new thing. It’s just... I suppose the piles are just different now. Instead of schoolwork, it's church bulletins. But the feeling of sitting there and not knowing how to start is exactly the same.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, were you often fidgety or unable to stay seated when you were supposed to?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's funny you ask that, because I think people always assume that little girls are perfectly still and quiet, but I... well, I was never really the type to be jumping off the furniture or running around the classroom, if that's what you mean. I wasn't getting up out of my seat and causing a ruckus like some of the little boys did. But still? Oh, I don't think I've ever been truly still a day in my life. \n\nEven now, at my age, I just cannot seem to sit quietly. I go to these committee meetings at the church—I'm on the altar guild, and we have these long meetings to discuss the flower arrangements and the linens, and they can go on for an hour or more. And I try, I really do try to just sit politely with my hands folded in my lap like a proper lady. But before I even realize I'm doing it, I'll catch myself rubbing my hands together over and over, or I'll be tapping my foot against the leg of the chair. Tap, tap, tap. And if I have a pen in my hand to take notes? Forget it. I'll be clicking the top of it, or rolling it between my fingers, or taking the cap off and putting it back on. \n\nAnd I shift in my chair constantly. I'll cross my legs, and then uncross them, and then lean to one side, and then adjust my skirt, and then lean forward. I just get this... this restless feeling in my body, like my muscles just can't settle down. And I know it's noticeable, because sometimes the other ladies will stop talking and look over at me, and I'll realize I've been jiggling my foot so hard that the whole table is shaking. It's terribly embarrassing, really. You'd think a seventy-year-old woman would know how to sit still through a meeting, but I just can't seem to manage it. I try to stop, I'll tell myself, \"Just be still,\" but then five minutes later, my foot is going again. It's like I have a little motor inside me that just never shuts off, even when I'm supposed to be perfectly still.\n\nDoctor: Have these same problems caused trouble for you in other parts of your life too, like work, managing the household, or relationships?\n\nPatient: Oh, yes. Oh, it really has. It breaks my heart, it truly does, because I’ve always tried to be so involved and useful, but it feels like it's just ruining everything lately. \n\nI mean... just to give you an example, just last month, I was supposed to be in charge of this big event at the senior center. It was their annual Spring Jubilee luncheon, which is a lovely gathering, we do a silent auction and a big catered meal to raise money for the community pantry. I was so honored when they asked me to chair the committee. But... well, I had all the vendor contacts, and the volunteer sign-up sheets, and the donation forms, and I just couldn't organize any of it. The papers were scattered all over the house, just like my kitchen table. I missed the deadline to order the centerpieces, and I kept forgetting to call the caterer back to confirm the final headcount because I couldn't find the list of who had RSVP'd. It got so chaotic, and I was so paralyzed by all the details, that I actually had to step down. I had to call the director and give up leading the whole event completely. It was just humiliating to admit I couldn't handle it. I felt like I let all those lovely people down.\n\nAnd it’s taking a toll on my family, too. Especially with my daughter, Sarah. She’s such a sweetheart, she really is, and she works so hard at her own job, but she's had to start stepping in to manage me, basically. It started a little while ago when a couple of important notices got buried under those church bulletins I mentioned, and now... well, now she comes over every single Sunday afternoon. She says she's just coming to visit and have tea, but I know exactly what she's really doing. She sits down at that messy table and she double-checks all of our bills and goes through every single one of my husband's and my appointments. She literally takes my calendar and cross-references it to make sure I haven't messed up the dates or forgotten to mail a check. \n\nShe does it because she just doesn’t trust that I can keep everything straight on my own anymore. And I can't even be angry with her for interfering, because she's right! I *can't* keep it straight. But it just feels awful, you know? To be seventy years old and have your own child double-checking your mail because you're too scatterbrained to run your own household. I used to manage everything when she was a little girl, and now I can't even be trusted to pay the water bill on time without supervision. It just feels like this... this inability to organize anything is just bleeding into absolutely every part of my life.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, no. I wouldn't say that at all. I mean, I get frustrated, certainly. Like I was just saying about Sarah having to check my mail and stepping down from the Spring Jubilee... those things make me feel terribly foolish and upset in the moment. I might even have a little cry about it in the bathroom, just out of pure frustration with myself. But depressed? No, I really don't think so. \n\nI still wake up looking forward to the day, you know? I still love going out to my garden in the mornings. I have these absolutely beautiful hydrangea bushes that my husband and I planted when we first moved into this house—oh, goodness, it must have been thirty-five years ago now, back when the neighborhood was still mostly empty lots and young families. And I go out there with my coffee every morning and I just love looking at the blooms, deciding which ones to cut to bring inside. And I still enjoy my friends! I meet the girls for lunch every Tuesday at the little bistro downtown, the one that makes that lovely spinach quiche, and we just talk and laugh for hours about everything under the sun. \n\nSo, no, I don't have that dark cloud hanging over me that people talk about when they have real depression. I'm not lying in bed unable to face the day. I *want* to face the day! I want to do all these things and be involved! It's just that when I try to actually do them, I get so terribly tangled up in the details and the paperwork and the organizing. It’s more of a... well, a deep exasperation with my own brain, if that makes sense, rather than a true sadness. I just wish I could be as capable as I feel on the inside, instead of constantly letting things slip through the cracks. But no, doctor, not depressed. Just... very, very annoyed with myself.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, goodness, no. No, my memory is actually sharp as a tack, thank heavens. I know people sometimes assume that because I'm seventy and my house is a bit of a disaster area with all the papers, that I must be losing my mind or getting dementia or something awful like that. But it's really not my memory at all! 

Like, if I have a conversation with someone, I remember every single detail. Just this past Tuesday, at that lunch I was telling you about with the girls? Susan was telling us about her grandson, David—he just moved out to Seattle for a new job, and I remembered to ask her how his drive across the country went, because I knew he was taking his golden retriever with him and stopping at Mount Rushmore. And I remembered that his new job is with some big aerospace firm, working on the navigation systems. I remember all of that perfectly! 

And it's the same with my own life. I don't forget that an event is happening, or that I had a conversation with my husband about fixing the leaky faucet in the downstairs half-bath. I remember exactly what we talked about. The problem is just... well, like with the bills that Sarah comes over to check. It’s not that I *forget* we have a water bill. I know exactly when it’s due, it's always the fifteenth of the month. But I’ll take the mail out of the box, and I’ll set it on the kitchen counter to go make a cup of tea, and then I’ll see a gardening catalog, and then the bill just gets buried under a stack of magazines and those church bulletins I mentioned. So I don't forget the bill exists, I just... I lose the physical envelope, or I get entirely sidetracked before I can sit down and write the check. 

So, no, I'm not forgetting recent events or what people say to me. If anything, my head is entirely too full of all the things I *know* I'm supposed to be doing, and I remember every single one of my failures to do them! It's just getting the actual tasks organized and executed that completely trips me up. But my memory itself? Oh, it's perfectly fine. I could tell you exactly what I wore to my high school graduation, and I could tell you exactly what the ladies and I discussed over our quiche two days ago. It's just the organizing that's the problem.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n6\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F09, F41.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, hello, doctor. It's nice to meet you, too. I... well, I came in today because I just can’t seem to get anything in order anymore. It's really gotten completely out of hand. Just to give you an example, if you were to walk into my house right now and look at my kitchen table, you wouldn't even be able to see the wood underneath. It is just completely covered in piles. \n\nI mean, there are my husband’s appointment papers—he's had quite a few lately, and they always hand you those thick printouts with the instructions and the dates, and I bring them home and just set them down. And then right on top of those are all the church flyers. I try to keep up with the Sunday social hour, so I have all these bulletins and announcements. Oh, and the senior-center notes! They send out these newsletters and activity calendars, and I keep meaning to go through them, but they just pile up right alongside all these half-finished lists I make. I'm always starting lists on the backs of envelopes to try and remember what to do, but I never finish them. \n\nAnd the worst part, the absolute most frustrating part, is that I will pull out a chair and sit there at that table, looking at this mountain of paper, and I just... I don't know which thing to do first. I sit there trying to figure out where to start, and I get so overwhelmed that I just stare at it, or I shuffle a few things around, and before I know it, the sun is going down and the whole day is just gone. Completely gone. And I just sit there feeling terrible about it, but I don't know how to fix it.\n\nDoctor: Has this trouble getting things organized and knowing where to start come on only in the last few years?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. No, it's not just the last few years at all. It feels like it’s been my entire life, really. I mean, my husband thinks it’s just me getting older, you know, because when you reach seventy you expect some things to slip a little bit. But if I’m being completely honest with myself—and with you—it’s always been exactly like this. \n\nI was actually just thinking about this the other day. I was going through an old cedar chest in the spare bedroom, trying to find a specific photo album, and I happened to come across a stack of my old report cards from elementary school. My mother had saved every single one of them in this little tied-up bundle. And I sat on the edge of the bed reading them, and it was just... well, it brought tears to my eyes, honestly, because it was exactly the same struggle. Even as a little girl, my teachers would write these notes to my parents, and they always said the exact same things. They wrote that I was a bright girl, very capable, but just so careless. That was the word they always used: \"careless.\" \n\nI was constantly forgetting my reading books, or I would actually do my homework and then leave it sitting right on the counter at home, which used to drive my mother up the wall. And the seatwork... oh, my goodness. We would have these little worksheets to do at our desks, and I would just sit there looking out the window, watching the other children or just daydreaming, and the teacher would always have to come over and tap on my desk. I needed constant reminders to just finish my seatwork, because if I didn't finish it, I wasn't allowed to go outside for recess. I would just sit there at that little wooden desk with the inkwell hole, feeling so frustrated because I desperately wanted to go outside and play, but I just couldn't seem to get my brain to focus on finishing the paper in front of me. \n\nSo, no, doctor, it’s definitely not a new thing. It’s just... I suppose the piles are just different now. Instead of schoolwork, it's church bulletins. But the feeling of sitting there and not knowing how to start is exactly the same.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, were you often fidgety or unable to stay seated when you were supposed to?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's funny you ask that, because I think people always assume that little girls are perfectly still and quiet, but I... well, I was never really the type to be jumping off the furniture or running around the classroom, if that's what you mean. I wasn't getting up out of my seat and causing a ruckus like some of the little boys did. But still? Oh, I don't think I've ever been truly still a day in my life. \n\nEven now, at my age, I just cannot seem to sit quietly. I go to these committee meetings at the church—I'm on the altar guild, and we have these long meetings to discuss the flower arrangements and the linens, and they can go on for an hour or more. And I try, I really do try to just sit politely with my hands folded in my lap like a proper lady. But before I even realize I'm doing it, I'll catch myself rubbing my hands together over and over, or I'll be tapping my foot against the leg of the chair. Tap, tap, tap. And if I have a pen in my hand to take notes? Forget it. I'll be clicking the top of it, or rolling it between my fingers, or taking the cap off and putting it back on. \n\nAnd I shift in my chair constantly. I'll cross my legs, and then uncross them, and then lean to one side, and then adjust my skirt, and then lean forward. I just get this... this restless feeling in my body, like my muscles just can't settle down. And I know it's noticeable, because sometimes the other ladies will stop talking and look over at me, and I'll realize I've been jiggling my foot so hard that the whole table is shaking. It's terribly embarrassing, really. You'd think a seventy-year-old woman would know how to sit still through a meeting, but I just can't seem to manage it. I try to stop, I'll tell myself, \"Just be still,\" but then five minutes later, my foot is going again. It's like I have a little motor inside me that just never shuts off, even when I'm supposed to be perfectly still.\n\nDoctor: Have these same problems caused trouble for you in other parts of your life too, like work, managing the household, or relationships?\n\nPatient: Oh, yes. Oh, it really has. It breaks my heart, it truly does, because I’ve always tried to be so involved and useful, but it feels like it's just ruining everything lately. \n\nI mean... just to give you an example, just last month, I was supposed to be in charge of this big event at the senior center. It was their annual Spring Jubilee luncheon, which is a lovely gathering, we do a silent auction and a big catered meal to raise money for the community pantry. I was so honored when they asked me to chair the committee. But... well, I had all the vendor contacts, and the volunteer sign-up sheets, and the donation forms, and I just couldn't organize any of it. The papers were scattered all over the house, just like my kitchen table. I missed the deadline to order the centerpieces, and I kept forgetting to call the caterer back to confirm the final headcount because I couldn't find the list of who had RSVP'd. It got so chaotic, and I was so paralyzed by all the details, that I actually had to step down. I had to call the director and give up leading the whole event completely. It was just humiliating to admit I couldn't handle it. I felt like I let all those lovely people down.\n\nAnd it’s taking a toll on my family, too. Especially with my daughter, Sarah. She’s such a sweetheart, she really is, and she works so hard at her own job, but she's had to start stepping in to manage me, basically. It started a little while ago when a couple of important notices got buried under those church bulletins I mentioned, and now... well, now she comes over every single Sunday afternoon. She says she's just coming to visit and have tea, but I know exactly what she's really doing. She sits down at that messy table and she double-checks all of our bills and goes through every single one of my husband's and my appointments. She literally takes my calendar and cross-references it to make sure I haven't messed up the dates or forgotten to mail a check. \n\nShe does it because she just doesn’t trust that I can keep everything straight on my own anymore. And I can't even be angry with her for interfering, because she's right! I *can't* keep it straight. But it just feels awful, you know? To be seventy years old and have your own child double-checking your mail because you're too scatterbrained to run your own household. I used to manage everything when she was a little girl, and now I can't even be trusted to pay the water bill on time without supervision. It just feels like this... this inability to organize anything is just bleeding into absolutely every part of my life.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, no. I wouldn't say that at all. I mean, I get frustrated, certainly. Like I was just saying about Sarah having to check my mail and stepping down from the Spring Jubilee... those things make me feel terribly foolish and upset in the moment. I might even have a little cry about it in the bathroom, just out of pure frustration with myself. But depressed? No, I really don't think so. \n\nI still wake up looking forward to the day, you know? I still love going out to my garden in the mornings. I have these absolutely beautiful hydrangea bushes that my husband and I planted when we first moved into this house—oh, goodness, it must have been thirty-five years ago now, back when the neighborhood was still mostly empty lots and young families. And I go out there with my coffee every morning and I just love looking at the blooms, deciding which ones to cut to bring inside. And I still enjoy my friends! I meet the girls for lunch every Tuesday at the little bistro downtown, the one that makes that lovely spinach quiche, and we just talk and laugh for hours about everything under the sun. \n\nSo, no, I don't have that dark cloud hanging over me that people talk about when they have real depression. I'm not lying in bed unable to face the day. I *want* to face the day! I want to do all these things and be involved! It's just that when I try to actually do them, I get so terribly tangled up in the details and the paperwork and the organizing. It’s more of a... well, a deep exasperation with my own brain, if that makes sense, rather than a true sadness. I just wish I could be as capable as I feel on the inside, instead of constantly letting things slip through the cracks. But no, doctor, not depressed. Just... very, very annoyed with myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble with your memory, like forgetting recent conversations or events?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. No, my memory is actually sharp as a tack, thank heavens. I know people sometimes assume that because I'm seventy and my house is a bit of a disaster area with all the papers, that I must be losing my mind or getting dementia or something awful like that. But it's really not my memory at all! \n\nLike, if I have a conversation with someone, I remember every single detail. Just this past Tuesday, at that lunch I was telling you about with the girls? Susan was telling us about her grandson, David—he just moved out to Seattle for a new job, and I remembered to ask her how his drive across the country went, because I knew he was taking his golden retriever with him and stopping at Mount Rushmore. And I remembered that his new job is with some big aerospace firm, working on the navigation systems. I remember all of that perfectly! \n\nAnd it's the same with my own life. I don't forget that an event is happening, or that I had a conversation with my husband about fixing the leaky faucet in the downstairs half-bath. I remember exactly what we talked about. The problem is just... well, like with the bills that Sarah comes over to check. It’s not that I *forget* we have a water bill. I know exactly when it’s due, it's always the fifteenth of the month. But I’ll take the mail out of the box, and I’ll set it on the kitchen counter to go make a cup of tea, and then I’ll see a gardening catalog, and then the bill just gets buried under a stack of magazines and those church bulletins I mentioned. So I don't forget the bill exists, I just... I lose the physical envelope, or I get entirely sidetracked before I can sit down and write the check. \n\nSo, no, I'm not forgetting recent events or what people say to me. If anything, my head is entirely too full of all the things I *know* I'm supposed to be doing, and I remember every single one of my failures to do them! It's just getting the actual tasks organized and executed that completely trips me up. But my memory itself? Oh, it's perfectly fine. I could tell you exactly what I wore to my high school graduation, and I could tell you exactly what the ladies and I discussed over our quiche two days ago. It's just the organizing that's the problem."
  }
]

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nWell, hello, doctor. It's nice to meet you, too. I... well, I came in today because I just can’t seem to get anything in order anymore. It's really gotten completely out of hand. It is just completely covered in piles. \n\nI mean, there are my husband’s appointment papers—he's had quite a few lately, and they always hand you those thick printouts with the instructions and the dates, and I bring them home and just set them down. And then right on top of those are all the church flyers. I try to keep up with the Sunday social hour, so I have all these bulletins and announcements. Oh, and the senior-center notes! They send out these newsletters and activity calendars, and I keep meaning to go through them, but they just pile up right alongside all these half-finished lists I make. I'm always starting lists on the backs of envelopes to try and remember what to do, but I never finish them. \n\nAnd the worst part, the absolute most frustrating part, is that I will pull out a chair and sit there at that table, looking at this mountain of paper, and I just... I don't know which thing to do first. I sit there trying to figure out where to start, and I get so overwhelmed that I just stare at it, or I shuffle a few things around, and before I know it, the sun is going down and the whole day is just gone. Completely gone.
